From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, um, I came in because this thing at work has gotten really out of hand. I’m a barber, and I used to be able to get through my day normally, but now whenever it comes to using the straight razor, I just… I can’t handle it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to avoid it. Like, I’ll switch clients with someone if I think they’re going to want a shave, or I’ll say I can’t find the blade, or that it’s missing, or I’ll put it in the drawer and act like I don’t know where it went. It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment I’m just desperate not to have to pick it up.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it—if there’s no excuse or someone’s watching or I have to finish the shave—I’m shaking the whole time. My hands, my whole body feels like it’s not steady, and I’m just trying to get through it without anyone noticing too much. It’s become a real problem, and honestly I’m embarrassed, but that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that causes you this anxiety, or are there other things at work or in your daily life that make you feel similarly?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors, trimmers, all of that — that doesn’t set me off the same way. I know, logically, it’s just a tool, and it’s one I trained with. I’ve used it before. It’s not like I don’t understand how it works.\n\nBut when that straight razor is sitting there open at my station, it’s like my mind just goes straight to, “I’m going to hurt somebody,” or “I’m going to cut myself badly.” Even if nothing is happening yet, just seeing it open makes everything feel dangerous. It feels way bigger than it should, like I can’t trust myself around it, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of the straight razor or the anxiety you experience because of it ever affect you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, it’s mostly a work thing because that’s where the razor is, and that’s where I’m expected to actually use it on someone. At home I’m not really around one in the same way, so I don’t get that same immediate reaction day to day.\n\nI guess sometimes I’ll think about work when I’m not there, like worrying ahead of time that tomorrow someone’s going to ask for a shave, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s not like random things outside of work set me off the same way. It’s really tied to that straight razor and having to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about the straight razor when you're not thinking about work or an upcoming shift?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least I haven’t noticed it coming up out of nowhere like that. It’s not like I’m sitting at home watching TV and suddenly the straight razor pops into my head for no reason.\n\nFor me it’s more connected to work, or to thinking about work. Like if I remember I have a shift the next day, or I start picturing the kinds of clients who might come in, then yeah, my mind can go there. But if I’m completely away from that context and not thinking about the shop, I don’t think it’s something that just randomly takes over. It’s pretty tied to the idea of having to use it.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety about using the straight razor affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really relationships in the sense of, like, my friends or family getting mad at me or anything. But daily life outside of work, it kind of spills over because work is such a big part of my life, you know?\n\nThe biggest thing is it’s been affecting my reputation and money. I’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber even when the client was technically in my chair. And regular clients have complained, not always directly to my face, but I hear about it or I can tell they’re annoyed. Like they came to me for a full service, and then I’m suddenly making excuses or saying, “Oh, he’s better with that,” and handing them to someone else. It’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd I backed out of a barber battle because they required razor detailing. That one really bothered me, because I wanted to do it. I had been talking about entering, and then when I realized the razor work was part of it, I just… I couldn’t go through with it. So it’s not like it’s only a little inconvenience. It’s starting to affect what opportunities I take and how people see me at work, and that kind of follows me home because I keep thinking about how I’m messing things up.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of feelings of embarrassment or anxiety related to your situation as a barber?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding, like, parties or seeing friends because of it, not exactly. It’s more that the embarrassment is tied to what’s happening at work, and then I carry that around with me.\n\nLike, I’ve lost tips because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber, and some regular clients have complained or seemed frustrated with me. That gets embarrassing because these are people who know me as a barber, and I’m supposed to be able to do the full service. So if I’m around people who ask how work is going, or if barber stuff comes up, I feel kind of ashamed and I don’t really want to talk about it.\n\nAnd I did back out of a barber battle because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding one client—it was an actual event I wanted to be part of, and I didn’t do it because of the razor part. So outside of work, I’m not completely isolating myself or anything, but the embarrassment definitely follows me, and it affects what barber-related events or opportunities I’ll put myself into.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, is your anxiety more about the possibility of hurting someone else or about something happening to you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, I’ve used it before, and I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. I’m around sharp stuff constantly. But when the straight razor is actually open at my station, it feels different. It’s like my brain goes, “This could go really wrong right now.”\n\nA big part of it is the fear that I’m going to hurt the person in the chair—like I’ll slip or my hand will move wrong and I’ll cut them badly. But there’s also this feeling that I could cut myself too, like on my finger or hand, and not just a little nick. In the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I might make a small mistake.” It feels like I’m about to seriously hurt somebody or myself, even though another part of me knows that sounds bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about the straight razor stays with you even after you've left work and are relaxing or doing other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, once I leave the shop, I’m not holding the razor anymore, so it’s not the exact same panic feeling in my body, but it stays in my head. I’ll be trying to relax or eat dinner or watch something, and I’ll start replaying the day—like, “Did that client notice I was making an excuse?” or “How many tips am I losing because I keep dodging these shaves?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not just a private fear at this point. Regulars have complained, or at least I’ve heard they weren’t happy, because they came to me expecting the full service and I passed the razor shave off to someone else. And then I go home thinking about how that makes me look, like I’m not capable of doing part of my own job.\n\nThe barber battle thing especially stuck with me after work. I backed out because razor detailing was required, and even when I was home I kept thinking about it, like, “Man, I actually wanted to do that, and I let this stop me.” So yeah, it follows me after I leave. It turns into this whole loop about money, reputation, clients being disappointed, and opportunities I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations that have nothing to do with work or using a straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I get nervous about normal life stuff like anybody, but that specific fear—the one that really hits me hard—is tied to the straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it sounds strange because I use sharp tools all day. Clippers, shears, all of that doesn’t set me off like this. I trained with the straight razor too, so it’s not like I don’t understand what it is or how it’s supposed to be used. But when that razor is actually open and sitting there at my station, it’s like something switches in my head. I start feeling like I’m about to seriously mess up—like I’m going to hurt the client or cut myself badly.\n\nSo outside of that situation, no, I don’t think I have the same kind of fear just randomly. It’s really that tool, in that moment, when I’m expected to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about other things in your life besides the straight razor and work-related situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I worry about regular stuff, like money sometimes, whether clients are happy, keeping my schedule full, normal adult things. But I wouldn’t say I’m just constantly worrying about everything across my life.\n\nIt’s more like the straight razor has become this specific thing that kind of spreads into work-related worries. So then I’m not only afraid in the moment with the razor, I’m also thinking about what it means for my job, my reputation, whether people are going to stop booking with me, stuff like that. But those worries all kind of trace back to the same issue.\n\nOutside of that, I don’t feel like I’m walking around worrying excessively about every little thing. It’s not like I’m lying awake every night about a hundred different areas of my life. It’s really centered on the razor and what happens because I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities you enjoy doing in your free time that you've stopped doing because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really like hobbies at home, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t stopped, like, watching shows or going out because of it. The bigger thing is stuff connected to barbering that I actually enjoy or care about.\n\nLike, I used to like doing the full service for clients, especially regulars who trusted me, and now I keep avoiding the razor shave part or handing it off to another barber. That’s been messing things up. I’ve lost tips over it, and I’ve had regular clients complain because they came in expecting me to do the whole thing and then I’m suddenly making excuses or passing them to someone else.\n\nAnd there was this barber battle I really wanted to do. I was excited about it, honestly. But when I found out razor detailing was required, I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work—it was something I wanted to challenge myself with and be proud of. So I guess I haven’t stopped free-time activities in general, but I have stopped doing barber-related things I enjoyed because of the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your anxiety about the straight razor is affecting your overall sense of well-being or ability to function outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly it affects me through work, but work is such a big part of my life that it doesn’t just stay there neatly, you know? It follows me around because I keep thinking about what I avoided or what I’m going to have to avoid next.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll get to the point where a client wants the razor shave or the cleanup with the straight razor, and I either make some excuse or I pass them off to another barber. And people notice. Regular clients especially notice, because they know what I used to do for them. I’ve had a few complain, not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it hits me. Like, they came to me for the full service, and then I’m acting like I suddenly can’t do part of my own job.\n\nAnd that makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. Less confident. It’s not just about the money, though losing tips definitely stings. It’s more that I start questioning myself outside of the shop too, like, “Am I falling off? Are people going to stop trusting me?” So it affects my overall well-being that way.\n\nAnd then there was that barber battle I backed out of because razor detailing was required. That one really bothered me because it was something I actually wanted to do. It wasn’t just a shift at work or a client appointment. It was something I was excited about, something that would’ve made me feel proud. But once I knew the razor part was involved, I just couldn’t go through with it. So yeah, I’d say it’s affecting how I function, even if the fear itself is tied to the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social gatherings or events outside of work because you're embarrassed about your situation with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really social gatherings in the normal sense, no. Like, I’m not skipping birthday parties or avoiding friends because I’m worried someone’s going to ask me about the straight razor.\n\nBut I do feel embarrassed, and it comes out more around barber-related events or anything where my work comes up. The main thing is still that I’ve been avoiding razor shaves at the shop or passing them off to other barbers, and that’s been noticeable. I’ve lost tips over it, and some regular clients have complained because they came in expecting me to do the full service like I used to. That’s embarrassing, because these are people who trusted me, and now I’m making excuses or handing them over to somebody else.\n\nAnd then with the barber battle… I backed out because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding a work task. It was an event I actually wanted to do, and I just couldn’t bring myself to go through with it once I knew the razor would be part of it. So I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding regular social stuff, but I am avoiding work-related events or opportunities where the razor is involved, partly because I’m embarrassed and partly because I don’t want people seeing me freeze up or avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than the straight razor and work when you're not thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, everybody worries about normal life stuff, right? Bills, family, whether I’m doing okay, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about a bunch of random things all the time.\n\nWhen my mind gets stuck, it’s usually tied back to the razor or work somehow. Like even if I’m at home and not physically at the shop, I might start thinking about a client I avoided, or whether someone noticed, or what’s going to happen the next time someone asks for that kind of service. So it can feel like I’m worrying outside of work, but the topic is still basically the same thing.\n\nIf you mean do I worry excessively about, like, every possible thing in my life even when it has nothing to do with barbering or the straight razor… no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more focused than that.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that, no. I mean… I’ve had nights where I’m lying there thinking about work and the razor pops into my head, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble sleeping exactly because of that.\n\nIt’s more like, if something happened that day — like if I dodged doing a shave or felt embarrassed about it — I might replay it a little before bed. But I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s actually messing up my sleep in a major way. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a clear pattern, because I’m not sure it is.\n\nSo… maybe it crosses my mind sometimes at night, but I haven’t noticed it being the main reason I can’t sleep or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about things unrelated to work or the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can worry about regular things like anyone does, but it doesn’t feel excessive or like my mind is jumping from one problem to another all day.\n\nWhen I’m really stuck on something, it almost always ends up circling back to work or the straight razor somehow. Like even if I start off thinking about money or my reputation or whether I’m letting people down, it’s usually connected to that situation. I haven’t noticed myself constantly worrying about unrelated stuff, like health, family, random accidents, or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say no — not excessively. It’s pretty focused, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I guess the honest answer is yes, it has. Maybe not in the way people think of, like I’m not saying I never enjoy anything at all, but barbering used to be one of the things I was proud of, you know? Like the whole craft of it, doing clean work, having regulars come back, getting that little extra tip because they trusted me and liked the finish. And now there’s this whole part of the job that I’m constantly trying to dodge.

I’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber if I can. And regular clients have complained, which feels awful, because these are people who used to specifically ask for me. It’s embarrassing when they notice I’m kind of steering them away from something, or when they end up with someone else and I’m just standing there pretending it’s no big deal.

And there was a barber battle I wanted to do — I was actually excited about it at first — but razor detailing was required, and I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work, it was something I would’ve enjoyed, something that made me feel like I was improving and competing and being part of the barber community. So yeah, it’s definitely affected my ability to participate in things I used to get something out of.

So outside of work, I’m not completely isolating myself or anything, but the embarrassment definitely follows me, and it affects what barber-related events or opportunities I’ll put myself into.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, is your anxiety more about the possibility of hurting someone else or about something happening to you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. It’s not like random things outside of work set me off the same way. It’s really tied to that straight razor and having to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about the straight razor when you're not thinking about work or an upcoming shift?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least I haven’t noticed it coming up out of nowhere like that. It’s not like I’m sitting at home watching TV and suddenly the straight razor pops into my head for no reason.\n\nFor me it’s more connected to work, or to thinking about work. Like if I remember I have a shift the next day, or I start picturing the kinds of clients who might come in, then yeah, my mind can go there. But if I’m completely away from that context and not thinking about the shop, I don’t think it’s something that just randomly takes over. It’s pretty tied to the idea of having to use it.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety about using the straight razor affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really relationships in the sense of, like, my friends or family getting mad at me or anything. But daily life outside of work, it kind of spills over because work is such a big part of my life, you know?\n\nThe biggest thing is it’s been affecting my reputation and money. I’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber even when the client was technically in my chair. And regular clients have complained, not always directly to my face, but I hear about it or I can tell they’re annoyed. Like they came to me for a full service, and then I’m suddenly making excuses or saying, “Oh, he’s better with that,” and handing them to someone else. It’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd I backed out of a barber battle because they required razor detailing. That one really bothered me, because I wanted to do it. I had been talking about entering, and then when I realized the razor work was part of it, I just… I couldn’t go through with it. So it’s not like it’s only a little inconvenience. It’s starting to affect what opportunities I take and how people see me at work, and that kind of follows me home because I keep thinking about how I’m messing things up.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of feelings of embarrassment or anxiety related to your situation as a barber?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding, like, parties or seeing friends because of it, not exactly. It’s more that the embarrassment is tied to what’s happening at work, and then I carry that around with me.\n\nLike, I’ve lost tips because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber, and some regular clients have complained or seemed frustrated with me. That gets embarrassing because these are people who know me as a barber, and I’m supposed to be able to do the full service. So if I’m around people who ask how work is going, or if barber stuff comes up, I feel kind of ashamed and I don’t really want to talk about it.\n\nAnd I did back out of a barber battle because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding one client—it was an actual event I wanted to be part of, and I didn’t do it because of the razor part. So outside of work, I’m not completely isolating myself or anything, but the embarrassment definitely follows me, and it affects what barber-related events or opportunities I’ll put myself into.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, is your anxiety more about the possibility of hurting someone else or about something happening to you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, I’ve used it before, and I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. I’m around sharp stuff constantly. But when the straight razor is actually open at my station, it feels different. It’s like my brain goes, “This could go really wrong right now.”\n\nA big part of it is the fear that I’m going to hurt the person in the chair—like I’ll slip or my hand will move wrong and I’ll cut them badly. But there’s also this feeling that I could cut myself too, like on my finger or hand, and not just a little nick. In the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I might make a small mistake.” It feels like I’m about to seriously hurt somebody or myself, even though another part of me knows that sounds bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about the straight razor stays with you even after you've left work and are relaxing or doing other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, once I leave the shop, I’m not holding the razor anymore, so it’s not the exact same panic feeling in my body, but it stays in my head. I’ll be trying to relax or eat dinner or watch something, and I’ll start replaying the day—like, “Did that client notice I was making an excuse?” or “How many tips am I losing because I keep dodging these shaves?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not just a private fear at this point. Regulars have complained, or at least I’ve heard they weren’t happy, because they came to me expecting the full service and I passed the razor shave off to someone else. And then I go home thinking about how that makes me look, like I’m not capable of doing part of my own job.\n\nThe barber battle thing especially stuck with me after work. I backed out because razor detailing was required, and even when I was home I kept thinking about it, like, “Man, I actually wanted to do that, and I let this stop me.” So yeah, it follows me after I leave. It turns into this whole loop about money, reputation, clients being disappointed, and opportunities I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations that have nothing to do with work or using a straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I get nervous about normal life stuff like anybody, but that specific fear—the one that really hits me hard—is tied to the straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it sounds strange because I use sharp tools all day. Clippers, shears, all of that doesn’t set me off like this. I trained with the straight razor too, so it’s not like I don’t understand what it is or how it’s supposed to be used. But when that razor is actually open and sitting there at my station, it’s like something switches in my head. I start feeling like I’m about to seriously mess up—like I’m going to hurt the client or cut myself badly.\n\nSo outside of that situation, no, I don’t think I have the same kind of fear just randomly. It’s really that tool, in that moment, when I’m expected to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about other things in your life besides the straight razor and work-related situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I worry about regular stuff, like money sometimes, whether clients are happy, keeping my schedule full, normal adult things. But I wouldn’t say I’m just constantly worrying about everything across my life.\n\nIt’s more like the straight razor has become this specific thing that kind of spreads into work-related worries. So then I’m not only afraid in the moment with the razor, I’m also thinking about what it means for my job, my reputation, whether people are going to stop booking with me, stuff like that. But those worries all kind of trace back to the same issue.\n\nOutside of that, I don’t feel like I’m walking around worrying excessively about every little thing. It’s not like I’m lying awake every night about a hundred different areas of my life. It’s really centered on the razor and what happens because I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities you enjoy doing in your free time that you've stopped doing because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really like hobbies at home, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t stopped, like, watching shows or going out because of it. The bigger thing is stuff connected to barbering that I actually enjoy or care about.\n\nLike, I used to like doing the full service for clients, especially regulars who trusted me, and now I keep avoiding the razor shave part or handing it off to another barber. That’s been messing things up. I’ve lost tips over it, and I’ve had regular clients complain because they came in expecting me to do the whole thing and then I’m suddenly making excuses or passing them to someone else.\n\nAnd there was this barber battle I really wanted to do. I was excited about it, honestly. But when I found out razor detailing was required, I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work—it was something I wanted to challenge myself with and be proud of. So I guess I haven’t stopped free-time activities in general, but I have stopped doing barber-related things I enjoyed because of the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your anxiety about the straight razor is affecting your overall sense of well-being or ability to function outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly it affects me through work, but work is such a big part of my life that it doesn’t just stay there neatly, you know? It follows me around because I keep thinking about what I avoided or what I’m going to have to avoid next.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll get to the point where a client wants the razor shave or the cleanup with the straight razor, and I either make some excuse or I pass them off to another barber. And people notice. Regular clients especially notice, because they know what I used to do for them. I’ve had a few complain, not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it hits me. Like, they came to me for the full service, and then I’m acting like I suddenly can’t do part of my own job.\n\nAnd that makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. Less confident. It’s not just about the money, though losing tips definitely stings. It’s more that I start questioning myself outside of the shop too, like, “Am I falling off? Are people going to stop trusting me?” So it affects my overall well-being that way.\n\nAnd then there was that barber battle I backed out of because razor detailing was required. That one really bothered me because it was something I actually wanted to do. It wasn’t just a shift at work or a client appointment. It was something I was excited about, something that would’ve made me feel proud. But once I knew the razor part was involved, I just couldn’t go through with it. So yeah, I’d say it’s affecting how I function, even if the fear itself is tied to the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social gatherings or events outside of work because you're embarrassed about your situation with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really social gatherings in the normal sense, no. Like, I’m not skipping birthday parties or avoiding friends because I’m worried someone’s going to ask me about the straight razor.\n\nBut I do feel embarrassed, and it comes out more around barber-related events or anything where my work comes up. I’ve lost tips over it, and some regular clients have complained because they came in expecting me to do the full service like I used to. I mean, everybody worries about normal life stuff, right? Bills, family, whether I’m doing okay, that kind of thing. I’m not totally sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it causing big problems with friends or family, at least not in a clear way.

Like, I don’t really go around talking to them about the straight razor stuff that much. With friends, same thing — if they ask how work is, I might just say it’s fine or that I’m tired. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, um, I came in because this thing at work has gotten really out of hand. I’m a barber, and I used to be able to get through my day normally, but now whenever it comes to using the straight razor, I just… I can’t handle it.\n\nI’ll do almost anything to avoid it. Like, I’ll switch clients with someone if I think they’re going to want a shave, or I’ll say I can’t find the blade, or that it’s missing, or I’ll put it in the drawer and act like I don’t know where it went. It sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, but in the moment I’m just desperate not to have to pick it up.\n\nAnd if I can’t get out of it—if there’s no excuse or someone’s watching or I have to finish the shave—I’m shaking the whole time. My hands, my whole body feels like it’s not steady, and I’m just trying to get through it without anyone noticing too much. It’s become a real problem, and honestly I’m embarrassed, but that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that causes you this anxiety, or are there other things at work or in your daily life that make you feel similarly?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. Like, I can use clippers all day, scissors, trimmers, all of that — that doesn’t set me off the same way. I know, logically, it’s just a tool, and it’s one I trained with. I’ve used it before. It’s not like I don’t understand how it works.\n\nBut when that straight razor is sitting there open at my station, it’s like my mind just goes straight to, “I’m going to hurt somebody,” or “I’m going to cut myself badly.” Even if nothing is happening yet, just seeing it open makes everything feel dangerous. It feels way bigger than it should, like I can’t trust myself around it, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of the straight razor or the anxiety you experience because of it ever affect you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, it’s mostly a work thing because that’s where the razor is, and that’s where I’m expected to actually use it on someone. At home I’m not really around one in the same way, so I don’t get that same immediate reaction day to day.\n\nI guess sometimes I’ll think about work when I’m not there, like worrying ahead of time that tomorrow someone’s going to ask for a shave, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s not like random things outside of work set me off the same way. It’s really tied to that straight razor and having to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about the straight razor when you're not thinking about work or an upcoming shift?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least I haven’t noticed it coming up out of nowhere like that. It’s not like I’m sitting at home watching TV and suddenly the straight razor pops into my head for no reason.\n\nFor me it’s more connected to work, or to thinking about work. Like if I remember I have a shift the next day, or I start picturing the kinds of clients who might come in, then yeah, my mind can go there. But if I’m completely away from that context and not thinking about the shop, I don’t think it’s something that just randomly takes over. It’s pretty tied to the idea of having to use it.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety about using the straight razor affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really relationships in the sense of, like, my friends or family getting mad at me or anything. But daily life outside of work, it kind of spills over because work is such a big part of my life, you know?\n\nThe biggest thing is it’s been affecting my reputation and money. I’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber even when the client was technically in my chair. And regular clients have complained, not always directly to my face, but I hear about it or I can tell they’re annoyed. Like they came to me for a full service, and then I’m suddenly making excuses or saying, “Oh, he’s better with that,” and handing them to someone else. It’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd I backed out of a barber battle because they required razor detailing. That one really bothered me, because I wanted to do it. I had been talking about entering, and then when I realized the razor work was part of it, I just… I couldn’t go through with it. So it’s not like it’s only a little inconvenience. It’s starting to affect what opportunities I take and how people see me at work, and that kind of follows me home because I keep thinking about how I’m messing things up.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or events outside of work because of feelings of embarrassment or anxiety related to your situation as a barber?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding, like, parties or seeing friends because of it, not exactly. It’s more that the embarrassment is tied to what’s happening at work, and then I carry that around with me.\n\nLike, I’ve lost tips because I keep dodging razor shaves or handing them off to another barber, and some regular clients have complained or seemed frustrated with me. That gets embarrassing because these are people who know me as a barber, and I’m supposed to be able to do the full service. So if I’m around people who ask how work is going, or if barber stuff comes up, I feel kind of ashamed and I don’t really want to talk about it.\n\nAnd I did back out of a barber battle because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding one client—it was an actual event I wanted to be part of, and I didn’t do it because of the razor part. So outside of work, I’m not completely isolating myself or anything, but the embarrassment definitely follows me, and it affects what barber-related events or opportunities I’ll put myself into.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, is your anxiety more about the possibility of hurting someone else or about something happening to you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, I’ve used it before, and I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. I’m around sharp stuff constantly. But when the straight razor is actually open at my station, it feels different. It’s like my brain goes, “This could go really wrong right now.”\n\nA big part of it is the fear that I’m going to hurt the person in the chair—like I’ll slip or my hand will move wrong and I’ll cut them badly. But there’s also this feeling that I could cut myself too, like on my finger or hand, and not just a little nick. In the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I might make a small mistake.” It feels like I’m about to seriously hurt somebody or myself, even though another part of me knows that sounds bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about the straight razor stays with you even after you've left work and are relaxing or doing other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, once I leave the shop, I’m not holding the razor anymore, so it’s not the exact same panic feeling in my body, but it stays in my head. I’ll be trying to relax or eat dinner or watch something, and I’ll start replaying the day—like, “Did that client notice I was making an excuse?” or “How many tips am I losing because I keep dodging these shaves?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not just a private fear at this point. Regulars have complained, or at least I’ve heard they weren’t happy, because they came to me expecting the full service and I passed the razor shave off to someone else. And then I go home thinking about how that makes me look, like I’m not capable of doing part of my own job.\n\nThe barber battle thing especially stuck with me after work. I backed out because razor detailing was required, and even when I was home I kept thinking about it, like, “Man, I actually wanted to do that, and I let this stop me.” So yeah, it follows me after I leave. It turns into this whole loop about money, reputation, clients being disappointed, and opportunities I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations that have nothing to do with work or using a straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I get nervous about normal life stuff like anybody, but that specific fear—the one that really hits me hard—is tied to the straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it sounds strange because I use sharp tools all day. Clippers, shears, all of that doesn’t set me off like this. I trained with the straight razor too, so it’s not like I don’t understand what it is or how it’s supposed to be used. But when that razor is actually open and sitting there at my station, it’s like something switches in my head. I start feeling like I’m about to seriously mess up—like I’m going to hurt the client or cut myself badly.\n\nSo outside of that situation, no, I don’t think I have the same kind of fear just randomly. It’s really that tool, in that moment, when I’m expected to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about other things in your life besides the straight razor and work-related situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I worry about regular stuff, like money sometimes, whether clients are happy, keeping my schedule full, normal adult things. But I wouldn’t say I’m just constantly worrying about everything across my life.\n\nIt’s more like the straight razor has become this specific thing that kind of spreads into work-related worries. So then I’m not only afraid in the moment with the razor, I’m also thinking about what it means for my job, my reputation, whether people are going to stop booking with me, stuff like that. But those worries all kind of trace back to the same issue.\n\nOutside of that, I don’t feel like I’m walking around worrying excessively about every little thing. It’s not like I’m lying awake every night about a hundred different areas of my life. It’s really centered on the razor and what happens because I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities you enjoy doing in your free time that you've stopped doing because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really like hobbies at home, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t stopped, like, watching shows or going out because of it. The bigger thing is stuff connected to barbering that I actually enjoy or care about.\n\nLike, I used to like doing the full service for clients, especially regulars who trusted me, and now I keep avoiding the razor shave part or handing it off to another barber. That’s been messing things up. I’ve lost tips over it, and I’ve had regular clients complain because they came in expecting me to do the whole thing and then I’m suddenly making excuses or passing them to someone else.\n\nAnd there was this barber battle I really wanted to do. I was excited about it, honestly. But when I found out razor detailing was required, I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work—it was something I wanted to challenge myself with and be proud of. So I guess I haven’t stopped free-time activities in general, but I have stopped doing barber-related things I enjoyed because of the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your anxiety about the straight razor is affecting your overall sense of well-being or ability to function outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly it affects me through work, but work is such a big part of my life that it doesn’t just stay there neatly, you know? It follows me around because I keep thinking about what I avoided or what I’m going to have to avoid next.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll get to the point where a client wants the razor shave or the cleanup with the straight razor, and I either make some excuse or I pass them off to another barber. And people notice. Regular clients especially notice, because they know what I used to do for them. I’ve had a few complain, not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it hits me. Like, they came to me for the full service, and then I’m acting like I suddenly can’t do part of my own job.\n\nAnd that makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. Less confident. It’s not just about the money, though losing tips definitely stings. It’s more that I start questioning myself outside of the shop too, like, “Am I falling off? Are people going to stop trusting me?” So it affects my overall well-being that way.\n\nAnd then there was that barber battle I backed out of because razor detailing was required. That one really bothered me because it was something I actually wanted to do. It wasn’t just a shift at work or a client appointment. It was something I was excited about, something that would’ve made me feel proud. But once I knew the razor part was involved, I just couldn’t go through with it. So yeah, I’d say it’s affecting how I function, even if the fear itself is tied to the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social gatherings or events outside of work because you're embarrassed about your situation with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really social gatherings in the normal sense, no. Like, I’m not skipping birthday parties or avoiding friends because I’m worried someone’s going to ask me about the straight razor.\n\nBut I do feel embarrassed, and it comes out more around barber-related events or anything where my work comes up. The main thing is still that I’ve been avoiding razor shaves at the shop or passing them off to other barbers, and that’s been noticeable. I’ve lost tips over it, and some regular clients have complained because they came in expecting me to do the full service like I used to. That’s embarrassing, because these are people who trusted me, and now I’m making excuses or handing them over to somebody else.\n\nAnd then with the barber battle… I backed out because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding a work task. It was an event I actually wanted to do, and I just couldn’t bring myself to go through with it once I knew the razor would be part of it. So I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding regular social stuff, but I am avoiding work-related events or opportunities where the razor is involved, partly because I’m embarrassed and partly because I don’t want people seeing me freeze up or avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than the straight razor and work when you're not thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, everybody worries about normal life stuff, right? Bills, family, whether I’m doing okay, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about a bunch of random things all the time.\n\nWhen my mind gets stuck, it’s usually tied back to the razor or work somehow. Like even if I’m at home and not physically at the shop, I might start thinking about a client I avoided, or whether someone noticed, or what’s going to happen the next time someone asks for that kind of service. So it can feel like I’m worrying outside of work, but the topic is still basically the same thing.\n\nIf you mean do I worry excessively about, like, every possible thing in my life even when it has nothing to do with barbering or the straight razor… no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more focused than that.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that, no. I mean… I’ve had nights where I’m lying there thinking about work and the razor pops into my head, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble sleeping exactly because of that.\n\nIt’s more like, if something happened that day — like if I dodged doing a shave or felt embarrassed about it — I might replay it a little before bed. But I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s actually messing up my sleep in a major way. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a clear pattern, because I’m not sure it is.\n\nSo… maybe it crosses my mind sometimes at night, but I haven’t noticed it being the main reason I can’t sleep or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about things unrelated to work or the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can worry about regular things like anyone does, but it doesn’t feel excessive or like my mind is jumping from one problem to another all day.\n\nWhen I’m really stuck on something, it almost always ends up circling back to work or the straight razor somehow. Like even if I start off thinking about money or my reputation or whether I’m letting people down, it’s usually connected to that situation. I haven’t noticed myself constantly worrying about unrelated stuff, like health, family, random accidents, or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say no — not excessively. It’s pretty focused, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety about the straight razor is significantly affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess the honest answer is yes, it has. Maybe not in the way people think of, like I’m not saying I never enjoy anything at all, but barbering used to be one of the things I was proud of, you know? Like the whole craft of it, doing clean work, having regulars come back, getting that little extra tip because they trusted me and liked the finish. And now there’s this whole part of the job that I’m constantly trying to dodge.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber if I can. And regular clients have complained, which feels awful, because these are people who used to specifically ask for me. It’s embarrassing when they notice I’m kind of steering them away from something, or when they end up with someone else and I’m just standing there pretending it’s no big deal.\n\nAnd there was a barber battle I wanted to do — I was actually excited about it at first — but razor detailing was required, and I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work, it was something I would’ve enjoyed, something that made me feel like I was improving and competing and being part of the barber community. So yeah, it’s definitely affected my ability to participate in things I used to get something out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety about the straight razor is causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it causing big problems with friends or family, at least not in a clear way.\n\nLike, I don’t really go around talking to them about the straight razor stuff that much. Some people close to me probably know I’ve been stressed about work, or they can tell I’m quieter after a bad day, but I haven’t had some major falling-out with anyone over it. It’s more… I might be distracted, or not in the mood to explain myself, and then I just keep it to myself.\n\nWith family, I’ll sometimes avoid getting into details because it feels embarrassing, and I don’t want them making a big deal out of it or giving advice that makes me feel worse. With friends, same thing — if they ask how work is, I might just say it’s fine or that I’m tired. So I guess it could affect how open I am, but I don’t know if I’d call that relationship problems exactly.\n\nSo… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in the same obvious way it affects work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The bigger thing is stuff connected to barbering that I actually enjoy or care about.\n\nLike, I used to like doing the full service for clients, especially regulars who trusted me, and now I keep avoiding the razor shave part or handing it off to another barber. That’s been messing things up. I’ve lost tips over it, and I’ve had regular clients complain because they came in expecting me to do the whole thing and then I’m suddenly making excuses or passing them to someone else.\n\nAnd there was this barber battle I really wanted to do. I was excited about it, honestly. But when I found out razor detailing was required, I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work—it was something I wanted to challenge myself with and be proud of. So I guess I haven’t stopped free-time activities in general, but I have stopped doing barber-related things I enjoyed because of the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your anxiety about the straight razor is affecting your overall sense of well-being or ability to function outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly it affects me through work, but work is such a big part of my life that it doesn’t just stay there neatly, you know? It follows me around because I keep thinking about what I avoided or what I’m going to have to avoid next.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll get to the point where a client wants the razor shave or the cleanup with the straight razor, and I either make some excuse or I pass them off to another barber. And people notice. Regular clients especially notice, because they know what I used to do for them. I’ve had a few complain, not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it hits me. Like, they came to me for the full service, and then I’m acting like I suddenly can’t do part of my own job.\n\nAnd that makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. Less confident. It’s not just about the money, though losing tips definitely stings. It’s more that I start questioning myself outside of the shop too, like, “Am I falling off? Are people going to stop trusting me?” So it affects my overall well-being that way.\n\nAnd then there was that barber battle I backed out of because razor detailing was required. That one really bothered me because it was something I actually wanted to do. It wasn’t just a shift at work or a client appointment. It was something I was excited about, something that would’ve made me feel proud. But once I knew the razor part was involved, I just couldn’t go through with it. So yeah, I’d say it’s affecting how I function, even if the fear itself is tied to the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social gatherings or events outside of work because you're embarrassed about your situation with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really social gatherings in the normal sense, no. Like, I’m not skipping birthday parties or avoiding friends because I’m worried someone’s going to ask me about the straight razor.\n\nBut I do feel embarrassed, and it comes out more around barber-related events or anything where my work comes up. The main thing is still that I’ve been avoiding razor shaves at the shop or passing them off to other barbers, and that’s been noticeable. I’ve lost tips over it, and some regular clients have complained because they came in expecting me to do the full service like I used to. That’s embarrassing, because these are people who trusted me, and now I’m making excuses or handing them over to somebody else.\n\nAnd then with the barber battle… I backed out because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding a work task. It was an event I actually wanted to do, and I just couldn’t bring myself to go through with it once I knew the razor would be part of it. So I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding regular social stuff, but I am avoiding work-related events or opportunities where the razor is involved, partly because I’m embarrassed and partly because I don’t want people seeing me freeze up or avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than the straight razor and work when you're not thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, everybody worries about normal life stuff, right? Bills, family, whether I’m doing okay, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about a bunch of random things all the time.\n\nWhen my mind gets stuck, it’s usually tied back to the razor or work somehow. Like even if I’m at home and not physically at the shop, I might start thinking about a client I avoided, or whether someone noticed, or what’s going to happen the next time someone asks for that kind of service. So it can feel like I’m worrying outside of work, but the topic is still basically the same thing.\n\nIf you mean do I worry excessively about, like, every possible thing in my life even when it has nothing to do with barbering or the straight razor… no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more focused than that.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that, no. I mean… I’ve had nights where I’m lying there thinking about work and the razor pops into my head, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble sleeping exactly because of that.\n\nIt’s more like, if something happened that day — like if I dodged doing a shave or felt embarrassed about it — I might replay it a little before bed. But I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s actually messing up my sleep in a major way. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a clear pattern, because I’m not sure it is.\n\nSo… maybe it crosses my mind sometimes at night, but I haven’t noticed it being the main reason I can’t sleep or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about things unrelated to work or the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s really the straight razor. I mean, I work around sharp stuff all day — clippers, trimmers, scissors, blades, all of that — and I don’t have the same reaction. Like, I can use clippers on someone’s hairline and be fine, I’m focused, I know what I’m doing.

But when the straight razor is open at my station, it’s different. Even though I know, logically, it’s just a tool I trained with, and I’ve used it before, my body doesn’t treat it that way. It feels like I’m suddenly holding something dangerous in a way I can’t control, like I’m about to cut the client or cut myself badly. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, because it’s part of the job and plenty of barbers use it every day without thinking twice, but for me, that specific razor just sets something off.

So no, it’s not every sharp object. It’s pretty much that straight razor, especially when it’s open and I’m expected to use it on someone.

It’s really tied to that straight razor and having to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about the straight razor when you're not thinking about work or an upcoming shift?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least I haven’t noticed it coming up out of nowhere like that. And regular clients have complained, not always directly to my face, but I hear about it or I can tell they’re annoyed. Like they came to me for a full service, and then I’m suddenly making excuses or saying, “Oh, he’s better with that,” and handing them to someone else. That gets embarrassing because these are people who know me as a barber, and I’m supposed to be able to do the full service. So if I’m around people who ask how work is going, or if barber stuff comes up, I feel kind of ashamed and I don’t really want to talk about it.\n\nAnd I did back out of a barber battle because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding one client—it was an actual event I wanted to be part of, and I didn’t do it because of the razor part. So outside of work, I’m not completely isolating myself or anything, but the embarrassment definitely follows me, and it affects what barber-related events or opportunities I’ll put myself into.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, is your anxiety more about the possibility of hurting someone else or about something happening to you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, honestly. I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly.\n\nLike, logically I know it’s just a tool. I trained with it, I’ve used it before, and I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. I’m around sharp stuff constantly. But when the straight razor is actually open at my station, it feels different. It’s like my brain goes, “This could go really wrong right now.”\n\nA big part of it is the fear that I’m going to hurt the person in the chair—like I’ll slip or my hand will move wrong and I’ll cut them badly. But there’s also this feeling that I could cut myself too, like on my finger or hand, and not just a little nick. In the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I might make a small mistake.” It feels like I’m about to seriously hurt somebody or myself, even though another part of me knows that sounds bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about the straight razor stays with you even after you've left work and are relaxing or doing other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, once I leave the shop, I’m not holding the razor anymore, so it’s not the exact same panic feeling in my body, but it stays in my head. I’ll be trying to relax or eat dinner or watch something, and I’ll start replaying the day—like, “Did that client notice I was making an excuse?” or “How many tips am I losing because I keep dodging these shaves?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it’s not just a private fear at this point. Regulars have complained, or at least I’ve heard they weren’t happy, because they came to me expecting the full service and I passed the razor shave off to someone else. And then I go home thinking about how that makes me look, like I’m not capable of doing part of my own job.\n\nThe barber battle thing especially stuck with me after work. I backed out because razor detailing was required, and even when I was home I kept thinking about it, like, “Man, I actually wanted to do that, and I let this stop me.” So yeah, it follows me after I leave. It turns into this whole loop about money, reputation, clients being disappointed, and opportunities I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety or fear in situations that have nothing to do with work or using a straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I get nervous about normal life stuff like anybody, but that specific fear—the one that really hits me hard—is tied to the straight razor.\n\nLike, I know it sounds strange because I use sharp tools all day. Clippers, shears, all of that doesn’t set me off like this. I trained with the straight razor too, so it’s not like I don’t understand what it is or how it’s supposed to be used. But when that razor is actually open and sitting there at my station, it’s like something switches in my head. I start feeling like I’m about to seriously mess up—like I’m going to hurt the client or cut myself badly.\n\nSo outside of that situation, no, I don’t think I have the same kind of fear just randomly. It’s really that tool, in that moment, when I’m expected to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about other things in your life besides the straight razor and work-related situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I worry about regular stuff, like money sometimes, whether clients are happy, keeping my schedule full, normal adult things. But I wouldn’t say I’m just constantly worrying about everything across my life.\n\nIt’s more like the straight razor has become this specific thing that kind of spreads into work-related worries. So then I’m not only afraid in the moment with the razor, I’m also thinking about what it means for my job, my reputation, whether people are going to stop booking with me, stuff like that. But those worries all kind of trace back to the same issue.\n\nOutside of that, I don’t feel like I’m walking around worrying excessively about every little thing. It’s not like I’m lying awake every night about a hundred different areas of my life. It’s really centered on the razor and what happens because I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities you enjoy doing in your free time that you've stopped doing because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really like hobbies at home, if that’s what you mean. I haven’t stopped, like, watching shows or going out because of it. The bigger thing is stuff connected to barbering that I actually enjoy or care about.\n\nLike, I used to like doing the full service for clients, especially regulars who trusted me, and now I keep avoiding the razor shave part or handing it off to another barber. That’s been messing things up. I’ve lost tips over it, and I’ve had regular clients complain because they came in expecting me to do the whole thing and then I’m suddenly making excuses or passing them to someone else.\n\nAnd there was this barber battle I really wanted to do. I was excited about it, honestly. But when I found out razor detailing was required, I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work—it was something I wanted to challenge myself with and be proud of. So I guess I haven’t stopped free-time activities in general, but I have stopped doing barber-related things I enjoyed because of the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your anxiety about the straight razor is affecting your overall sense of well-being or ability to function outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly it affects me through work, but work is such a big part of my life that it doesn’t just stay there neatly, you know? It follows me around because I keep thinking about what I avoided or what I’m going to have to avoid next.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll get to the point where a client wants the razor shave or the cleanup with the straight razor, and I either make some excuse or I pass them off to another barber. And people notice. Regular clients especially notice, because they know what I used to do for them. I’ve had a few complain, not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it hits me. Like, they came to me for the full service, and then I’m acting like I suddenly can’t do part of my own job.\n\nAnd that makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. Less confident. It’s not just about the money, though losing tips definitely stings. It’s more that I start questioning myself outside of the shop too, like, “Am I falling off? Are people going to stop trusting me?” So it affects my overall well-being that way.\n\nAnd then there was that barber battle I backed out of because razor detailing was required. That one really bothered me because it was something I actually wanted to do. It wasn’t just a shift at work or a client appointment. It was something I was excited about, something that would’ve made me feel proud. But once I knew the razor part was involved, I just couldn’t go through with it. So yeah, I’d say it’s affecting how I function, even if the fear itself is tied to the razor.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social gatherings or events outside of work because you're embarrassed about your situation with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: Not really social gatherings in the normal sense, no. Like, I’m not skipping birthday parties or avoiding friends because I’m worried someone’s going to ask me about the straight razor.\n\nBut I do feel embarrassed, and it comes out more around barber-related events or anything where my work comes up. The main thing is still that I’ve been avoiding razor shaves at the shop or passing them off to other barbers, and that’s been noticeable. I’ve lost tips over it, and some regular clients have complained because they came in expecting me to do the full service like I used to. That’s embarrassing, because these are people who trusted me, and now I’m making excuses or handing them over to somebody else.\n\nAnd then with the barber battle… I backed out because razor detailing was required. That felt like more than just avoiding a work task. It was an event I actually wanted to do, and I just couldn’t bring myself to go through with it once I knew the razor would be part of it. So I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding regular social stuff, but I am avoiding work-related events or opportunities where the razor is involved, partly because I’m embarrassed and partly because I don’t want people seeing me freeze up or avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things other than the straight razor and work when you're not thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, everybody worries about normal life stuff, right? Bills, family, whether I’m doing okay, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about a bunch of random things all the time.\n\nWhen my mind gets stuck, it’s usually tied back to the razor or work somehow. Like even if I’m at home and not physically at the shop, I might start thinking about a client I avoided, or whether someone noticed, or what’s going to happen the next time someone asks for that kind of service. So it can feel like I’m worrying outside of work, but the topic is still basically the same thing.\n\nIf you mean do I worry excessively about, like, every possible thing in my life even when it has nothing to do with barbering or the straight razor… no, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more focused than that.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that, no. I mean… I’ve had nights where I’m lying there thinking about work and the razor pops into my head, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble sleeping exactly because of that.\n\nIt’s more like, if something happened that day — like if I dodged doing a shave or felt embarrassed about it — I might replay it a little before bed. But I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s actually messing up my sleep in a major way. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a clear pattern, because I’m not sure it is.\n\nSo… maybe it crosses my mind sometimes at night, but I haven’t noticed it being the main reason I can’t sleep or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about things unrelated to work or the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can worry about regular things like anyone does, but it doesn’t feel excessive or like my mind is jumping from one problem to another all day.\n\nWhen I’m really stuck on something, it almost always ends up circling back to work or the straight razor somehow. Like even if I start off thinking about money or my reputation or whether I’m letting people down, it’s usually connected to that situation. I haven’t noticed myself constantly worrying about unrelated stuff, like health, family, random accidents, or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say no — not excessively. It’s pretty focused, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety about the straight razor is significantly affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess the honest answer is yes, it has. Maybe not in the way people think of, like I’m not saying I never enjoy anything at all, but barbering used to be one of the things I was proud of, you know? Like the whole craft of it, doing clean work, having regulars come back, getting that little extra tip because they trusted me and liked the finish. And now there’s this whole part of the job that I’m constantly trying to dodge.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber if I can. And regular clients have complained, which feels awful, because these are people who used to specifically ask for me. It’s embarrassing when they notice I’m kind of steering them away from something, or when they end up with someone else and I’m just standing there pretending it’s no big deal.\n\nAnd there was a barber battle I wanted to do — I was actually excited about it at first — but razor detailing was required, and I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work, it was something I would’ve enjoyed, something that made me feel like I was improving and competing and being part of the barber community. So yeah, it’s definitely affected my ability to participate in things I used to get something out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety about the straight razor is causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it causing big problems with friends or family, at least not in a clear way.\n\nLike, I don’t really go around talking to them about the straight razor stuff that much. Some people close to me probably know I’ve been stressed about work, or they can tell I’m quieter after a bad day, but I haven’t had some major falling-out with anyone over it. It’s more… I might be distracted, or not in the mood to explain myself, and then I just keep it to myself.\n\nWith family, I’ll sometimes avoid getting into details because it feels embarrassing, and I don’t want them making a big deal out of it or giving advice that makes me feel worse. With friends, same thing — if they ask how work is, I might just say it’s fine or that I’m tired. So I guess it could affect how open I am, but I don’t know if I’d call that relationship problems exactly.\n\nSo… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in the same obvious way it affects work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using other sharp objects, or is it only the straight razor that causes you anxiety?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I mean, I work around sharp stuff all day — clippers, trimmers, scissors, blades, all of that — and I don’t have the same reaction. Like, I can use clippers on someone’s hairline and be fine, I’m focused, I know what I’m doing.\n\nBut when the straight razor is open at my station, it’s different. Even though I know, logically, it’s just a tool I trained with, and I’ve used it before, my body doesn’t treat it that way. It feels like I’m suddenly holding something dangerous in a way I can’t control, like I’m about to cut the client or cut myself badly. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, because it’s part of the job and plenty of barbers use it every day without thinking twice, but for me, that specific razor just sets something off.\n\nSo no, it’s not every sharp object. It’s pretty much that straight razor, especially when it’s open and I’m expected to use it on someone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more focused than that.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your anxiety about the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that, no. I mean… I’ve had nights where I’m lying there thinking about work and the razor pops into my head, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble sleeping exactly because of that.\n\nIt’s more like, if something happened that day — like if I dodged doing a shave or felt embarrassed about it — I might replay it a little before bed. But I haven’t really been keeping track of whether it’s actually messing up my sleep in a major way. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a clear pattern, because I’m not sure it is.\n\nSo… maybe it crosses my mind sometimes at night, but I haven’t noticed it being the main reason I can’t sleep or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry excessively about things unrelated to work or the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can worry about regular things like anyone does, but it doesn’t feel excessive or like my mind is jumping from one problem to another all day.\n\nWhen I’m really stuck on something, it almost always ends up circling back to work or the straight razor somehow. Like even if I start off thinking about money or my reputation or whether I’m letting people down, it’s usually connected to that situation. I haven’t noticed myself constantly worrying about unrelated stuff, like health, family, random accidents, or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say no — not excessively. It’s pretty focused, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety about the straight razor is significantly affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess the honest answer is yes, it has. Maybe not in the way people think of, like I’m not saying I never enjoy anything at all, but barbering used to be one of the things I was proud of, you know? Like the whole craft of it, doing clean work, having regulars come back, getting that little extra tip because they trusted me and liked the finish. And now there’s this whole part of the job that I’m constantly trying to dodge.\n\nI’ve lost tips because I’ll avoid doing razor shaves, or I’ll pass them off to another barber if I can. And regular clients have complained, which feels awful, because these are people who used to specifically ask for me. It’s embarrassing when they notice I’m kind of steering them away from something, or when they end up with someone else and I’m just standing there pretending it’s no big deal.\n\nAnd there was a barber battle I wanted to do — I was actually excited about it at first — but razor detailing was required, and I backed out. That one bothered me a lot because it wasn’t just work, it was something I would’ve enjoyed, something that made me feel like I was improving and competing and being part of the barber community. So yeah, it’s definitely affected my ability to participate in things I used to get something out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety about the straight razor is causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it causing big problems with friends or family, at least not in a clear way.\n\nLike, I don’t really go around talking to them about the straight razor stuff that much. Some people close to me probably know I’ve been stressed about work, or they can tell I’m quieter after a bad day, but I haven’t had some major falling-out with anyone over it. It’s more… I might be distracted, or not in the mood to explain myself, and then I just keep it to myself.\n\nWith family, I’ll sometimes avoid getting into details because it feels embarrassing, and I don’t want them making a big deal out of it or giving advice that makes me feel worse. With friends, same thing — if they ask how work is, I might just say it’s fine or that I’m tired. So I guess it could affect how open I am, but I don’t know if I’d call that relationship problems exactly.\n\nSo… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in the same obvious way it affects work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about using other sharp objects, or is it only the straight razor that causes you anxiety?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I mean, I work around sharp stuff all day — clippers, trimmers, scissors, blades, all of that — and I don’t have the same reaction. Like, I can use clippers on someone’s hairline and be fine, I’m focused, I know what I’m doing.\n\nBut when the straight razor is open at my station, it’s different. Even though I know, logically, it’s just a tool I trained with, and I’ve used it before, my body doesn’t treat it that way. It feels like I’m suddenly holding something dangerous in a way I can’t control, like I’m about to cut the client or cut myself badly. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, because it’s part of the job and plenty of barbers use it every day without thinking twice, but for me, that specific razor just sets something off.\n\nSo no, it’s not every sharp object. It’s pretty much that straight razor, especially when it’s open and I’m expected to use it on someone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
